Algorithm 8.1

Computing the separator

The following lemmas summarize useful properties of
Separator(
X,\gamma
)
.

35.4
[
98
]
.

A
q
-separator
S
of a graph
G
with
|V|=n
vertices contains at most
\frac{n}{q}
vertices,
i.e.,
|S|<\frac{n}{q}
.

35.5
[
98
]
.

Let
X
be a strongly connected set of vertices with
|X|=k
, let
r\in X
, and let
\gamma
be an integer such that
q=\lfloor\gamma/(2\log k)\rfloor\geq 1
. Then
Separator(
X,\gamma
)

computes a
\lfloor\gamma/(2\log k)\rfloor
-separator
if there exists a vertex
v\in X
where the distance
between
r
and
v
is at least
\gamma
. If no
such vertex
v
exists, then
Separator(
X,\gamma
)
returns the empty set.

The following lemma bounds the symbolic resources of the algorithm.

35.6
.

Algorithm
8.1
runs in
O(|X|)
symbolic operations and uses
O(1)
symbolic space.

Proof.

The bound on the symbolic operations of the while loop at Line
8.1
is clearly
in
O(|X|)
because we perform
\mathit{Post}_{E}(K)\cap X
operations until the set
K
fully contains

X
or
\mathit{Post}_{E}(K)\cap X
is fully contained in
K
. Note that we only perform a constant amount
of symbolic work in the body of the while-loop.
As each
\mathit{Post}_{E}(K)\cap X
operation adds at least one vertex to
X
until termination, we perform

O(|X|)
many operations in total.
A similar argument holds for the while-loop at Line
8.1
.
Note that we use a constant amount of sets in Algorithm
8.1
which implies

O(1)
symbolic space.
∎

36
Symbolic MEC decomposition

In this section, we first define how we collapse end-components.
Then we present the algorithm for the symbolic MEC decomposition.

36.1
Collapsing End-components

A key concept in our algorithm is to collapse a detected EC
X\subseteq V
of an MDP
P=(V,E,\langle V_{1},V_{R}\rangle,\delta)
to a single player-1 vertex
v\in X
in order to speed up the computation of end-components that contain
X
.
Notice that we do not have access to the graph directly, but only have access to the graph through symbolic operations.

Procedure

CollapseEC(
X,P
)

1

if

X\cap V_{1}\neq\emptyset

then

2

v\leftarrow\mathit{Pick}(X\cap V_{1})

3

4

E\leftarrow E\cup\left(((\mathit{Pre}_{E}(X)\setminus X)\times\{v\})\cup(\{v\}\times(\mathit{Post}_{E}(X)\setminus X))\right)
;
//
v
gets edges of
X

5

E\leftarrow E\setminus\left((V\times(X\setminus v))\cup((X\setminus v)\times V)\right);
//
Remove all other edges of
X

return

\{v\}

else

E\leftarrow E\setminus\left(X\times X\right)

6

//
Remove all edges of
X

Algorithm 8.2

Collapse an EC
X
into single vertex

We define the collapsing (see Algorithm
8.2
) of an EC
X
as picking a player-1
vertex
v\in X
, directing all the incoming edges
X
to
v
, directing the outgoing edges of
X
from
v
and removing all edges to and from vertices in
X\setminus v
. Also, we do not include edges going from
X
to
v
.
For vertices not in
X
, we have that they are in a non-trivial MEC in the modified MDP
iff they are in a non-trivial MEC in the original MDP.
We denote with
\mathsf{EC}(P)
the set of sets with all end-components in
P
.
The following lemma summarizes the property as observed in
[
78
]
.

36.1
.

For MDP
P=(V,E,\langle V_{1},V_{R}\rangle,\delta)
with
X\in\mathsf{EC}(P)

and the MDP
P^{\prime}
that results from collapsing
X
to
v\in X
with
CollapseEC(
X,P
)
we have:
(a) for
D\subseteq V\setminus X
we have
D\in\mathsf{EC}(P)
iff
D\in\mathsf{EC}(P^{\prime})
;
(b) for
D\in\mathsf{EC}(P)
with
D\cap X\not=\emptyset
we have
(D\setminus X)\cup\{v\}\in\mathsf{EC}(P^{\prime})
;
and (c) for
D\in\mathsf{EC}(P^{\prime})
with
v\in D
we have
D\cup X\in\mathsf{EC}(P)
.

Procedure

SymbolicMEC(
(P^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},\langle V^{\prime}_{1},V^{\prime}_{R}\rangle,\delta^{\prime}),\gamma)
)

1

M\leftarrow\emptyset,\textsc{MECs}\leftarrow\emptyset,P\leftarrow P^{\prime}

2

3

while

C\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$V,\emptyset,P^{\prime}$}}\textnormal{{)}}

do

//
compute vertices in non-trivial MECs of
C

4

M\leftarrow M\cup\textnormal{{SymMEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$C,\gamma,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}
//
uses P instead of P’

5

while

C\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$M,\emptyset,P^{\prime}$}}\textnormal{{)}}

do

//
compute non-trivial MECs

6

\textsc{MECs}\leftarrow\textsc{MECs}\cup\{C\}

return

MECs

Algorithm 8.3

Computing the MEC decomposition of an MDP

36.2
.

Note that we modify the set of edges
E
of
P
using basic set operations only and that such operations
are supported by all standard symbolic tools like BDDs.
After the update, we then use the new set
E
for all subsequent
\mathit{Pre}(.)
and
\mathit{Post}(.)
operations.

36.2
Algorithm Description

The input to our algorithm is an MDP
P^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},\langle V_{1}^{\prime},V_{R}^{\prime}\rangle,\delta^{\prime})
.
In the first stage, we compute the set
M\subseteq V
of all vertices that are in a non-trivial MEC,
and then, in the second stage, we use this set
M

to compute the MECs with an SCC algorithm (see Algorithm
8.3
).

In the first stage, we iteratively compute the SCCs

C_{1},\dots,C_{\ell}
of
P^{\prime}
and immediately apply
SymMEC(
\cdot
)
(cf. Algorithm
8.4
) to
C_{i}

to compute the vertices
M_{i}
of
C_{i}
that are in a non-trivial MEC.
The set
M
is the union over these sets, i.e.,
M=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq\ell}M_{i}
.

SymMEC(
\cdot
)
applies collapsing operations and thus modifies the edge set of the MDP.
We thus hand a copy
P
of the original MDP
P^{\prime}
to
SymMEC(
\cdot
)
.
Finally, to obtain all non-trivial MECs in
P^{\prime}
we restrict the graph to the vertices in
M
and compute the SCCs which correspond to the MECs. Note that trivial MECs are player-1 vertices that are not contained in any non-trivial MEC and thus can be simply computed
by iterating over the vertices of
V_{1}\setminus M
.

In the following we focus on the function
SymMEC(
\cdot
)
which is the core of our algorithm.
To this end, we introduce the operation
\mathit{ROut}(S)=\mathit{Pre}_{E}(V\setminus S)\cap(S\cap V_{R})
,
that computes the set of random vertices in
S
with edges to
V\setminus S
.

The function
SymMEC(
\cdot
)
.

SymMEC(
\cdot
)
works recursively: The input is a set
S
of strongly connected
vertices and a parameter
\gamma
for separator computations that is fixed over all recursive calls.
The main idea is to compute a separator to divide the original graph into smaller SCCs, recursively compute the vertices
which are in MECs of the smaller SCCs, and
then compute the MECs of the original graph by incrementally adding the vertices of the separator
back into the recursively computed MEC decomposition.
In each recursive call, we first check whether the given set
S
is larger than one
(if not it cannot be a non-trivial MEC) and then
if
S
is a non-trivial EC by checking if
\mathit{ROut}(S)=\emptyset
.
If
S
is a non-trivial EC we collapse
S
by calling Algorithm
8.2

and the algorithm then returns
M=S
.
If
\mathit{ROut}(S)\neq\emptyset
,
S
is not a non-trivial EC but it may contain nontrivial ECs of
C
.
We then try to compute a balanced separator
T
of
S
which is nonempty
only if the diameter of
S
is large enough (
\geq 2\gamma
).
We further distinguish between the two cases:
In the first case, we succeed to compute the balanced separator and
we recurse on the strongly connected components in
\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(T))
.

After computing and collapsing the ECs in
\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(T))

we incrementally add vertices of
T
to
S
and compute the ECs of
S\setminus T
until
T
is empty.
In each incremental step, we add one vertex
v
to
S\setminus T
and find the SCC of
v
in
S\setminus T
.
If the random attractor of
\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime})
(note that this computation now considers all vertices in
P
)
does not contain the whole SCC
S^{\prime}
we are able to prove that we can identify a new EC of
C
.

Otherwise, the vertex
v
does not create a new EC in
S\setminus T
.
In the second case where we fail to compute the balanced separator we know
that the diameter of
S
is small (
<2\gamma
). We remove the
random attractor
X
of
\mathit{ROut}(S)
(this set cannot contain ECs due to Lemma
35.3
),
recompute the strongly connected components in the set
S\setminus X
and recurse on
each of them one after the other.

Figure
36.2
illustrates one recursive call of the
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
function:
In the first step, we compute that
\mathit{ROut}(S)
is nonempty, i.e.,
S
cannot be a MEC.
We then successfully compute a separator
T
with parameter
\gamma
of the strongly
connected graph
S
and recurse on the SCCs of
S\setminus\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(T)
(orange dottet circles).
In the second step, we recursively identify two nontrivial MECs and add them to
M
. Next,
we perform the incremental iterations for all vertices in
T

to identify the MECs containing the vertices in
\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(T)
, (i.e., also in
T
).
In step three, we pick
w\in T
, remove it
from
T
and compute its SCC
S^{\prime}=S\setminus T
. In step four, we compute the random attractor of

\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime})
, i.e.,

\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime}))=\{w\}
. Because it contains
w
, there is no new MEC containing
w
.
In step five, we remove
x
from
T
and compute its SCC
S^{\prime}
. This time, the random attractor
of
\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime})
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime}))
, does not contain
x
and the SCC
S^{\prime}
of

x
without
\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime}))
has size greater one. We add the vertices in
S^{\prime}\setminus\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime}))
to
M
. In step six, we do the last incremental
iteration, identify no new nontrivial MECs and return
M
.

Algorithm
8.4
illustrates the pseudocode of
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
.

36.3
.

Note that our symbolic algorithm does not require randomization which is in contrast to the best
known MEC decomposition algorithms for the standard RAM model
[
31
]
.
The latter algorithms rely on decremental SCCs algorithms that are randomized as they maintain
ES-trees
[
127
]
from randomly chosen centers. Instead, our symbolic algorithm relies on a
deterministic incremental approach.

Procedure

SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)

1

M\leftarrow\emptyset

2

if

|S|\leq 1

then

return

M

3

if

\mathit{ROut}(S)=\emptyset

then

//
check if
S
is an EC

4

CollapseEC(
S,P
)

5

return

M\cup S

6

(T=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{t}\})\leftarrow\textnormal{{Separator(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S,\gamma$}}\textnormal{{)}}

7

if

T\neq\emptyset

then

//
T\neq\emptyset
if
\mathsf{diam}(S)\geq 2\gamma

8

A\leftarrow\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(T)\cap S

9

while

S_{j}\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S\setminus A,\emptyset,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

do

10

M\leftarrow M\cup\textnormal{{SymMEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S_{j},\gamma,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

while

T\neq\emptyset

do

//
incremental EC detection for
v\in T

11

v\leftarrow\mathit{Pick}(T)

12

T\leftarrow T\setminus\{v\}

13

S^{\prime}\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S\setminus T,\{v\},P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

14

if

|S^{\prime}|=1

then

continue

15

//
EC is trivial

Z\leftarrow\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S^{\prime}))
;
//
consider vertices in
P

S^{\prime}\leftarrow S^{\prime}\setminus Z

16

if

S^{\prime}\neq\emptyset

then

//
S^{\prime}
contains a non-trivial EC

17

U\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S^{\prime},\{v\},P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

18

CollapseEC(
U,P
)

19

M\leftarrow M\cup U

20

return

M

21

else

//
T=\emptyset
and thus
\mathsf{diam}(S)<2\gamma

22

X\leftarrow\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S))

23

while

S_{j}\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S\setminus X,\emptyset,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

do

24

M\leftarrow M\cup\textnormal{{SymMEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S_{j},\gamma,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

return

M

25

Algorithm 8.4

Compute the ECs of an SCS recursively

36.3
Correctness

We first consider the correctness of Algorithm
8.4
which will then imply the correctness
of Algorithm
8.3
.
To this end, consider an MDP
P=(V,E,\langle V_{1},V_{R}\rangle,\delta)
and an SCC
C
of
P
.
The non-trivial end-components of a subset
S
of
V
are given by
\mathsf{EC}(S)
and
the set of vertices that appear in non-trivial (maximal) ECs of
C
are denoted by

M_{C}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{EC}(C)}Q
.

We first observe that in all calls to
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
(see Algorithm
8.4
.) the set

S
is strongly connected.

36.4
.

For a strongly connected set
S\subseteq C
we have
that in the computation of
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)

for all the calls
SymMEC(
S_{j},\gamma,P
)
the set

S_{j}
is strongly connected and
S_{j}\subseteq S
.

Proof.

If
|S|=1
or
\mathit{ROut}(S)=\emptyset
there are no recursive calls the statement is true.
Now consider the case where
T\not=\emptyset
.
At Line
8.4
, we consider
S_{j}
which is an SCC in
the graph
S\setminus(T\cup A)
, obviously a subset of
S
and strongly connected.
Now consider the case where
T=\emptyset
.
At Line
8.4
we consider
S_{j}
which is an SCC of
S\setminus X
, obviously a subset of
S
and strongly connected.
∎

Let
M
be the set returned by
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
.
Note that the ultimate goal of Algorithm
8.4
is to compute
M_{C}
for a given SCC
C
of
P^{\prime}
.
The next lemma shows that every call of Algorithm
8.4
with a strongly connected set
S
returns the
set
M_{S}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{EC}(S)}Q
and collapses all ECs
Q\in\mathsf{EC}(S)
in
P
.

36.5
.

Let
S
be a strongly connected set of vertices and
M
be the set returned by
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
, then for all non-trivial ECs
Q\in\mathsf{EC}(S)
in
P^{\prime}
we have
Q\subseteq M
and
Q
is collapsed in
P
.

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathsf{EC}(C)
with
Q\subseteq S
.
We prove the statement by induction on the size of
S
.
For the base case, i.e., if
S
is empty or contains only one vertex,
Algorithm
8.4
returns the empty-set at Line
8.4
and thus the condition holds.

For the inductive step, let
|S|>1
.
If
Q=S
, we detect it at Line
8.4

and return
S
at Line
8.4
.
Then we collapse the set of vertices at Line
8.4
.
Thus,
M
contains
Q
and the claim holds.
We distinguish two cases concerning
T
at Line
8.4
:
First if we have non-empty
T
also the random attractor
A
of
T
at Line
8.4

is non-empty.
Thus the SCCs of
S\setminus A
are strictly smaller than
S
.
Thus, we can use the induction hypothesis to stipulate that if
Q
is contained
in one of the SCCs computed at Line
8.4

we have
Q\subseteq M
after the while-loop at Lines
8.4
-
8.4
.
Additionally, by the induction hypothesis, they are collapsed into a player-1 vertex.
Also, if
Q
contains a subset
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
that is a non-trivial EC
and contained in one of the SCCs of
S\setminus A
,
we have that
Q^{\prime}\subseteq M
after the while-loop at Lines
8.4
-
8.4
.
Again, by induction hypothesis,
Q^{\prime}
is collapsed into a player-1 vertex.
We proceed by proving that the separator
T
must contain one of the vertices of
Q
.

36.6
.

For any non-trivial EC
Q\in\mathsf{EC}(S)
which is not fully contained
in one of the SCCs computed at Line
8.4
we have
T\cap Q\not=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
Q
be an arbitrary EC in
\mathsf{EC}(S)
.
Observe that if
Q
is not fully contained in an SCC of
S\setminus A

(computed at Line
8.4
), we have
Q\cap A\neq\emptyset
.
We now show that
Q\cap T\neq\emptyset
:
Assume the contrary, i.e.,
Q\cap T=\emptyset
.
Consequently,
Q
is a strongly connected set in
S\setminus T
and not
fully contained in an SCC of
S\setminus A
.
Also,
Q
contains a vertex of
A\setminus T
.
Let
v\in Q\cap(A\setminus T)
such that there is no
v^{\prime}\in Q\cap(A\setminus T)
which
is on a lower level of the attractor
A
.
If
v\in V_{1}
, by the definition of the attractor and the above assumption, all its outing edges leave the set
Q
, which is in contradiction to
Q
being strongly connected.
Thus we have
v\in V_{R}
and, by the definition of the attractor,
it must have an edge to a smaller level and by the above assumption the target vertex is not in
Q
.
That is
Q
has an outgoing random edge which contradicts the assumption that
Q
is an EC. ∎

The following claim shows an invariant for the while-loop at Line
8.4
.

36.7
.

For iteration
j\geq 0
of the while-loop at Line
8.4
holds: There are no non-trivial ECs
E\subseteq S\setminus T
in
P
.

Proof.

The induction base
j=0
holds
for the following reasons:
A\setminus T
cannot include
Q
due to Claim
36.6
.

S\setminus A
cannot contain
Q

due to the fact that
Q
must contain a vertex
in
T
and that we collapsed the ECs contained in SCCs computed at
Line
8.4
.
For the induction step, assume that there are no non-trivial ECs in
S\setminus T

before iteration
j=\ell
.
When we include one vertex
v
of
T
into
S

at Line
8.4
there can only be one new non-trivial EC
Q_{v}
in
S
,
i.e., the one including
v
, as the remaining graph is unchanged.
We argue that if such an EC
Q_{v}
exists it is removed before iteration
\ell+1
of the while-loop
at Line
8.4
:
The EC
Q_{v}
must be strongly connected, and thus it is contained in the SCC
S^{\prime}
of
v

at Line
8.4
. Note, that if
|S^{\prime}|=1
, the new EC is trivial, which
concludes the proof. Otherwise, we compute
Z
at Line
8.4
, which does not
contain a vertex of
Q_{v}
by Lemma
35.3
and remove it from
S^{\prime}
.
If
S^{\prime}
is non-empty, it contains exactly one non-trivial SCC as we argued above.
That is the SCC
Q_{v}
and we thus add
Q_{v}
to
M
and collapse it.
As a consequence there is no non-trivial EC in
S\setminus T
left.
Thus the claim holds.
∎

It remains to show that Algorithm
8.4
finds the EC
Q

which contains vertices in
S\setminus M
.
Consider the first iteration where
Q\subseteq S\setminus T
.
We show that
Q\setminus M=\emptyset
after this iteration:
Because
Q
might contain non-trivial ECs
Q^{\prime}_{i}
that were already collapsed due to
the recursive call, or due to prior iteration of the while-loop at Line
8.4
,
it follows from Lemma
36.1
that there exists an EC
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
in
P
such that
Q^{\prime}\supset(Q\setminus M)

and
Q^{\prime}
contains one or more vertices corresponding to the collapsed sub-ECs of
Q
.
Due to Claim
36.7
we have that
Q^{\prime}
is collapsed in the current iteration
and thus
Q^{\prime}
is added to
M
, i.e., we have that
Q\subseteq M
and that
Q
is collapsed.

Now consider the case
T=\emptyset
(i.e.,
\mathsf{diam}(S)<2\gamma
).
As
\mathit{ROut}(S)\not=\emptyset
we have that the set
X
computed at Line
8.4
is non-empty.
Note that
Q
cannot contain a vertex in
X
by Lemma
35.3

and the fact that
S
is strongly connected by Lemma
36.4
.
Consequently,
Q
is contained in one of the SCCs of
S\setminus X
.
Note that each such SCC has size strictly smaller than
S
due to the fact that
X
is non-empty.
The claim then holds by the induction hypothesis.
This completes the proof of Lemma
36.5
.
∎

By the above lemma, we have that the algorithm finds all ECs.
We next show that all vertices added to
M
are contained
in some EC.

36.8
.

The set
M
is a subset of
M_{S}
, i.e.,
M\subseteq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{EC}(S)}Q
.

Proof.

We show the claim by induction over the size of
S
.
For the base case, i.e.,
|S|\leq 1
the claim holds trivially because we return
the empty set at Line
8.4
and any set of size less than two only contains
trivial ECs.
For the inductive step, let
|S|>1
.
For the return statement at Line
8.4
we argue as follows:
Because
S
has no random vertices with edges out of
S

(considering
P^{\prime}
, Line
8.4
),
S
is a non-trivial EC.
Thus, in Line
8.4
we correctly return
S
.

For the case
T\not=\emptyset
we argue as follows:
Let
T
,
A
be the separator and its attractor as computed in Line
8.4
and
Line
8.4
. Note that for all SCCs
S_{j}
in the graph
S\setminus A

we have
|S_{j}|<|S|
because
|A|>0
. Thus, by induction hypothesis, all vertices added
in the recursive call at Line
8.4
are in
M_{S}
.
We claim that for each iteration of the while loop in Line
8.4

we add only non-trivial ECs to
M
:
Note that by Claim
36.7
there is no non-trivial EC at the start of the
while loop in Line
8.4
.
Let
v
be the vertex we choose to remove from
T
at Line
8.4
.
Let
S_{v}
be the SCC of
v
computed at Line
8.4
.
If
|S_{v}|=1
we continue to the next iteration without adding anything to
M
and
the claim holds.
Otherwise, there are two cases based on the
computation of the attractor
Z
of random vertices
with edges out of
S_{v}
at Line
8.4
:
If
Z
contains
S_{v}
, we do not add vertices to
M
and the claim holds.
If
S_{v}\setminus Z\neq\emptyset
we add the SCC
S^{\prime}_{v}
of
v
in
S_{v}\setminus Z
to
M
.
It remains to show that the non-trivial SCC
S^{\prime}_{v}
as computed at Line
8.4
is a non-trivial EC:
Note that for all
v\in S^{\prime}_{v}\cap V_{1}
we have
\mathit{Out}(v)\cap S^{\prime}_{v}\neq\emptyset
, otherwise

v\in Z
. Also, for all
u\in S^{\prime}_{v}\cap V_{R}
we must have
\mathit{Out}(u)\subseteq S^{\prime}_{v}
, otherwise

u\in Z
. Thus,
S_{v}
is an EC.

For the case
T=\emptyset
note that each SCC found at Line
8.4
must be of size strictly
less than
|S|
because
|X|\geq 1
.
Thus, by induction hypothesis, the vertices added at Line
8.4

are in
M_{S}
.
∎

Lemma
36.5
and Lemma
36.8
imply the correctness of
Algorithm
8.4
and Algorithm
8.3
.

36.9
Correctness.

Given an SCC
S
of an MDP, Algorithm
8.4
returns the set
M_{C}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{EC}(C)}Q
, i.e., the set of vertices that are contained in a non-trivial MEC of
S
.

36.10
Correctness.

Given an MDP
P^{\prime}
, Algorithm
8.3
returns the set of non-trivial MECs of
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Due to Proposition
36.9

M
contains all vertices in nontrivial ECs.
It remains to show that the nontrivial MECs are the SCCs of
V\cap M
.
By definition, each nontrivial EC is strongly connected. Towards a contradiction assume
that a nontrivial MEC
Q
is not an SCC but part of some larger SCC
C
of
V\cap M
(there is
an SCC which is not a MEC).
But then, by the definition of
M
, we have that
C
is the union of several MECs, i.e., it is strongly connected and has no random outgoing edges and thus
C
is an
EC
. This is in contradiction to
Q
being a MEC.
Thus, each nontrivial MEC is an SCC and, by the definition of
M
, the union of the MECs covers

M
. Thus there are no further SCCs.
∎

36.4
Symbolic Operations Analysis

We first bound the total number of symbolic operations for computing the separator
T
at
Line
8.4
, recursing upon the SCCs
S\setminus T
at Line
8.4
and
adding the vertices in
T
back to compute the rest of the ECs of
S
at
Lines
8.4
–
8.4
during all calls to Algorithm
8.4
.

36.11
.

The total number of symbolic operations of Lines
8.4
–
8.4

in all calls to Algorithm
8.4
is in
O\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor}\right)
.

Proof.

In Lemma
35.6
we proved that computing the separator at
Line
8.4
takes
O(|S|)
symbolic operations.
The same holds for computing the attractor at Line
8.4
due to
Lemma
35.2
and computing the SCCs at Line
8.4
.
Each iteration of the while-loop at Line
8.4

takes
O(|S|)
symbolic operations: Computing the SCC of
v
twice can be done in
O(|S|)
symbolic operations
due to Theorem
35.1
. Similarly, computing the random attractor at
Line
8.4
is in
O(|S|)
symbolic operations due to Lemma
35.2
.
The remaining lines can be done in a constant amount of symbolic operations.
It remains to bound the symbolic operations of the while-loop at Line
8.4
where
we call Algorithm
8.4
recursively at Line
8.4
for each
SCC in
S\setminus A
. Note that the size of
T
determines the number of iterations the while-loop
at Line
8.4
has, and how big the SCCs in
S\setminus A
are.
We obtain that
|T|
is of size at most

\frac{|S|}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log|S|)\rfloor}
combining
Observation
35.4
and Lemma
35.5
.
Due to the argument above the following equation bounds the
running time of Lines
8.4
–
8.4
for some constant
c
which is greater than the number of constant symbolic operations in
SymMEC(
C,\gamma,P
)
if
|S|\geq\gamma
.

F(S)\leq|T|\cdot|S|\cdot c+\max_{|T|=1\dots\frac{|S|}{\lfloor(\gamma/2\log{n})\rfloor}}\sum_{S_{i}\in\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus T)}F(S_{i})

If
|S|<\gamma
we only have the costs for computing the separator and thus
F(S)\leq c\cdot|S|
.
Next, we prove the bound in Claim
36.12
.

36.12
.

F(S)\in O\left(\frac{|S|^{2}}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log{|S|})\rfloor}+|S|\right)
.

Proof.

We prove the inequality by induction on the size of
S
.
That is we show
F(S)\leq\frac{|S|^{2}}{Z}c^{\prime}
where
Z=\lfloor\gamma/(2\log|S|)\rfloor
for some
c^{\prime}>c
.
Obviously the inequality is true for
|S|<\gamma
, i.e., the base case is true.
For the inductive step, consider

F(S)\leq\frac{|S|^{2}}{Z}c^{\prime}
for
|S|\geq\gamma
.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{S_{i}\in\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus T)}F(S_{i})\leq\sum_{S_{i}\in\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus T)}c^{\prime}(|S_{i}|^{2}/(\lfloor\gamma/(2\log|S_{i}|)\rfloor)+|S_{i}|)\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}|S|+\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{S_{i}\in\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus T)}\hskip-5.0ptc^{\prime}|S_{i}|^{2}/Z\leq c^{\prime}|S|+\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{S_{i}\in\mathtt{SCCs}(S\setminus T)}\hskip-5.0ptc^{\prime}|S_{i}|\cdot(|S|-|T|Z)/Z\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}|S|+c^{\prime}(|S|-|T|)(|S|-|T|Z)/Z

The first inequality is due to the induction hypothesis and the third inequality is
due to the fact that we have a
Z
-separator and Lemma
35.5
.
It remains to add
|S||T|c
:

\displaystyle F(S)\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}|S|+c^{\prime}(|S|-|T|)(|S|-|T|Z)/Z+|S||T|c\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}|S|+c^{\prime}/Z(|S|^{2}-|S||T|-|T||S|Z+|T||S|)+|S||T|c=

\displaystyle c^{\prime}|S|+|S|^{2}/Zc^{\prime}-|S||T|c^{\prime}+|S||T|c\leq c^{\prime}|S|+\frac{|S|^{2}}{Z}c^{\prime}.

The first inequality is due to the fact that
|T|\leq|S|/Z
(Observation
35.4
).
This concludes our proof by induction of
F(S)\leq c^{\prime}\cdot(\frac{|S|^{2}}{Z}+|S|)
.
∎

Now using that claim and
|S|\leq n
we obtain a
O\left(n^{2}/\lfloor\gamma/(2\log{n})\rfloor+n\right)
bound which can further be simplified to
O\left(n^{2}/\lfloor\gamma/(2\log{n})\rfloor\right)
by using
\gamma\leq n
.
∎

The second part of our analysis bounds the symbolic operations of the case when

\mathsf{diam}(S)<2\gamma
at Lines
8.4
–
8.4
and the work done from Line
8.4
to Line
8.4
.

36.13
.

The total number of symbolic operations of Lines
8.4
–
8.4

in all calls to Algorithm
8.4
is in
O(n\cdot\gamma+\frac{n^{2}}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor})
.

Proof.

If a vertex is in the set
X
at Line
8.4
it is not recursed upon or ever looked at again,
thus we charge the symbolic operations of all attractor computations to the vertices in the attractor.
This adds up to a total of
O(n)
symbolic operations by Lemma
35.2
.
Additionally, note that
|X|
is non-empty, as otherwise
|S|
is declared as EC in the
if-condition at Line
8.4
. Thus, Lines
8.4
–
8.4

occur at most
n
times.
The number of symbolic operations for computing SCCs is in time
O(\sum_{C\in\mathtt{SCCs}(G)}(D_{C}+1))
due to
Theorem
35.1
. We can distribute the costs the SCCs according to the diameters of the SCCs. We provide separate arguments for counting the costs for SCCs
S_{j}
with
\mathsf{diam}(S_{j})<2\gamma

and SCCs
S_{j}
with
\mathsf{diam}(S_{j})\geq 2\gamma
.
First, we consider the costs for SCCs
S_{j}
with
\mathsf{diam}(S_{j})<2\gamma
.
Computing the SCC
S_{j}
costs
O(\gamma)
operations and
the algorithm either terminates in the next step or at least one vertex is removed from the SCC via a separator or attractor.
That is we have at most
n
such SCCs computations and thus an overall cost of
O(n\gamma)
.
Now we consider the costs for SCCs
S_{j}
with
\mathsf{diam}(S_{j})\geq 2\gamma
.
Whenever computing such an SCC, we simply charge all its vertices for the costs of computing the SCC,
i.e.,
O(1)
for each vertex.
For such an SCC the algorithm either terminates in the next step or a separator is computed and removed.
We thus have that each vertex is charged again after at least
\lfloor\gamma/(2\log|S|)\rfloor

many nodes are removed from its SCC. In total, each vertex is charged at most
\frac{|S|}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log|S|)\rfloor}
many times.
We get an
O(|S|\frac{|S|}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log|S|)\rfloor})
upper bound for the SCCs with large diameter,
∎

Putting Lemma
36.11
and Lemma
36.13
together,
we obtain the
O(n\cdot\gamma+\frac{n^{2}}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor})
bound for Algorithm
8.4

which also applies to Algorithm
8.3
as the SCC-computations only require
O(n)
operations.

36.14
.

Algorithm
8.4
and Algorithm
8.3
both have
O(n\cdot\gamma+\frac{n^{2}}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor})
symbolic operations.

36.5
Symbolic Space

Symbolic space usage counted as the maximum number of sets (and not their size) at any point in time
is a crucial metric and limiting factor of symbolic computation in practice
[
107
]
.
In this section, we consider the symbolic space usage of Algorithm
8.4
, highlight a key issue, and present a solution to the issue.

Key Issue.

Even though Algorithm
8.3
beats the current best
symbolic
Algorithm for computing
the MEC decomposition in the number of symbolic operations (current best:
O(n\sqrt{m})
, space:
O(\sqrt{n})

[
93
]
),
without further improvements, Algorithm
8.4
requires
O(n)
symbolic space as we discuss in the following.
First, note that each call of
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
, when excluding the sets stored by recursive calls, only stores a constant number of sets and requires a logarithmic number of sets to execute
SCCFind(
\cdot
)
.
That is, the recursion depth is the crucial factor here.
As we show below, by the
\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor
-separator property, the recursion depth due to the case
T\not=\emptyset
is
O\left(n/\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor\right)
.
However, the case
T=\emptyset
might lead to a recursion depth of
O(n)
when
in each iteration only a constant number of vertices is removed
and the diameter of the resulting SCC is still smaller than
2\gamma
.
As Algorithm
8.4
uses a constant amount of sets for each recursive call,
it uses
\widetilde{O}(n)
space in total.

Reducing the symbolic space.

We resolve the above space issue by modifying Algorithm
8.4
for the case
T=\emptyset
(see Algorithm
8.5
.):
At the while loop at Line
8.4
we first consider the SCCs
S_{j}

with less than
|S|/2
vertices and recurse on them.
If there is an SCC
S^{\prime}
with more than
|S|/2
vertices we process it at the end, i.e., we use one additional set to store that SCC until the SCC algorithm terminates.
As now all the computations of the current call to
SymMEC(
S,\gamma,P
)
are done we can
simply reuse the sets of the current calls to start the computation for
S^{\prime}
.
We do so by setting
S
to
S^{\prime}
and continuing in the Line 1 of Algorithm
8.4
.
Using that we only recurse on sets which are of size
\leq|S|/2

and thus get a recursion depth of
O(\log n)
for this case.
Moreover, the modified algorithm has the same computation operations as the original one and thus
the bounds for the number of symbolic operations apply as well.

else

//
T=\emptyset
and thus
\mathsf{diam}(S)<2\gamma

23

X\leftarrow\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathit{ROut}(S))
;
S_{tmp}=\emptyset

24

while

S_{j}\leftarrow\textnormal{{SCCFind(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S\setminus X,\emptyset,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

do

25

if

S_{j}\geq|S|/2
and
|S_{j}|>1

then

S_{tmp}\leftarrow S_{j}
;
continue

26

M\leftarrow M\cup\textnormal{{SymMEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$S_{j},M,\gamma,P$}}\textnormal{{)}}

if

S_{tmp}\neq\emptyset

then

S\leftarrow S_{tmp}
; goto Line 3

27

return

M

28

Algorithm 8.5

Reduced Space Version of Algorithm
8.4

36.15
.

The maximum recursion depth of the modified algorithm is in

O(\frac{n}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor}+\log n)
.

Proof.

Consider the recursion occurring due to Line
8.4
.
Because
T
is a
\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor
-separator, an SCC in
S\setminus T

contains at most
n-\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor\cdot|T|\leq n-\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor

vertices as
|T|\geq 1
. We determine how often we can remove

\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor
from
n
until there are no vertices
left which gives the recursion depth
k
. It follows that
k=\frac{n}{\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor}
.
Now consider the recursion occurring due to Line
8.4
. In the modified version we have that
|S_{j}|<|S|/2

and thus this kind of recursion is bounded by
O(\log n
).
∎

Algorithm
8.4
has
O(n\gamma+n^{2}/\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor)
many symbolic
operations due to Lemma
36.11
and Lemma
36.13
and the number of sets is
in
O(n/\lfloor\gamma/(2\log n)\rfloor+\log n)
due to Lemma
36.15
.
In symbolic algorithms it is of particular interest to optimize symbolic space resources. Note that we obtain
a space-time trade-off when setting the parameter
\gamma
such that
(2\sqrt{n}+2)\log n\leq\gamma\leq(2n+1)\log n
.

For the symbolic space of Algorithm
8.3
notice that the SCC algorithms are in logarithmic symbolic space and the algorithm itself only needs to stores the set
M
and the current SCC.
Thus, when we immediately output the computed MECs it only requires
O(\log n)
additional space.

36.16
.

The MEC decomposition of an MDP can be computed in
O\left(n^{2-\epsilon}\log n\right)

symbolic operations and with symbolic space
O\left(n^{\epsilon}\log n\right)
for
0<\epsilon\leq 0.5
.

By setting
\epsilon=0.5
we obtain
that the MEC decomposition of an MDP can be computed in
\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{n})
symbolic operations and with symbolic space
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
.

37
Symbolic Qualitative Analysis of Parity Objectives

In this section, we present symbolic algorithms for the qualitative analysis of parity objectives.

37.1
[
113
,
8
]
.

For all MDPs
P
, and all parity objectives
\text{Parity}(p)
, there exists a memoryless strategy

\sigma
such that for all
v\in\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Parity}(p))
we have
\Pr_{v}^{\sigma}(\text{Parity}(p))=1
.

37.1
Almost-sure Reachability.

In this section, we present a symbolic algorithm that computes reachability
objectives
\text{Reach}(T)
in an MDP.
The algorithm is a symbolic version of
[
75
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Symbolic Graph Reachability.

For a graph
G=(V,E)
and a set of vertices
S
, the set

\mathit{GraphReach}(S,G)
is the set of vertices
V
that can reach a vertex of
S
within
G
. We compute it
by repeatedly calling
S\leftarrow\mathit{Pre}_{E}(S)
until we reach a fixed point.
In the worst case, we add one vertex in each such call and need
|\mathit{GraphReach}(S,G)\setminus S|+1=O(n)
many
\mathit{Pre}_{E}(\cdot)
operations to reach a fixed point.

Algorithm Description.

Given an MDP
P
we compute the set
\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Reach}(T,P))
as follows:
First, if player 1 can reach one vertex of a MEC he can reach all the vertices of a MEC and thus
we can collapse each MEC
M
to a player-1 vertex.
If
M
contains a vertex of
T
, we include the collapsed vertex into
T
.

P^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},\langle V^{\prime}_{1},V^{\prime}_{2}\rangle,\delta^{\prime})
is the MDP where the MECs of
P
are collapsed as described above.
Next, we compute the set of vertices
S
which can reach
T
in the graph induced by
P^{\prime}
.
A vertex in
V^{\prime}\setminus S
cannot reach
T
almost-surely because there is no path to
T
.
Note that a play starting from a vertex in the random attractor
A
of
V^{\prime}\setminus S
might also end up in
V^{\prime}\setminus S
. We
thus remove
A
from
V^{\prime}
to obtain the set
R
, where vertices can almost-surely
reach
T
in
P^{\prime}
. Finally, to transfer the result back to
P
we include all MECs with a vertex in
R
.

We implement Algorithm
8.3
to compute the MEC decomposition of
P
but note that we could use any symbolic MEC algorithm.
To minimize the extra space usage we also assume that the algorithm that computes the
MEC decomposition outputs one MECs after the
other instead of all MECs at once.
Note that we can easily modify Algorithm
8.3
to output one MEC after the
other by iteratively returning each SCC found at Line
8.3
.
Moreover, we only require logarithmic space to maintain the state of the SCC
algorithm
[
74
]
.

Input:

An MDP
P=(V,E,\langle V_{1},V_{R}\rangle,\delta)
and a target set
T\subseteq V

Output:

\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Reach}(T,P))

Procedure

SymASReach(
T,\gamma,P
)

1

V^{\prime}\leftarrow V,E^{\prime}\leftarrow E,V^{\prime}_{1}\leftarrow V_{1},V^{\prime}_{R}\leftarrow V_{R},\delta^{\prime}\leftarrow\delta

2

P^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},\langle V^{\prime}_{1},V^{\prime}_{R}\rangle,\delta^{\prime})

3

for

M\leftarrow\mathsf{ComputeMECs}(P)

do

4

C\leftarrow\textnormal{{CollapseEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$M,P^{\prime}$}}\textnormal{{)}}

5

if

M\cap T\neq\emptyset

then

T\leftarrow T\cup C

6

S\leftarrow\mathit{GraphReach}(T,P^{\prime})
;
A\leftarrow\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P^{\prime}}(V^{\prime}\setminus S)
;
R\leftarrow V^{\prime}\setminus A

7

8

for

M\leftarrow\mathsf{ComputeMECs}(P)

do

9

if

M\cap R\neq\emptyset

then

R\leftarrow R\cup M

10

//
If
v\in M
can reach
T
,
M
can reach
T
.

11

return

R

12

13

Algorithm 8.6

Symbolic Almost-Sure Reachability

We prove the following two propositions for Algorithm
8.6
.
Let
P
be an MDP,
T
a set of vertices and
\mathbf{MEC}

be the number of symbolic operations we need to compute the MEC decomposition.
Let
\mathit{space}(\mathbf{MEC})
denote the space of computing the MEC decomposition.

37.2
Correctness
[
74
]
.

Algorithm
8.6
computes the set
\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Reach}(T))
.

37.3
Running time and Space.

The total number of symbolic operations of Algorithm
8.6
is
O(\mathbf{MEC}+n)
. Algorithm
8.6
uses
\widetilde{O}(\mathit{space}(\mathbf{MEC}))
symbolic space.

Proposition
37.3
and Proposition
37.2
together with Theorem
36.16
yield the following theorem.

37.4
.

The set
\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Reach}(T))
of an MDP can be computed with
\widetilde{O}(n^{2-\epsilon})

many symbolic operations and
\widetilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})

symbolic space for
0<\epsilon\leq 0.5
.

37.2
Parity Objectives.

In this section, we consider the qualitative analysis of MDPs with parity objectives.
We present an algorithm for computing the winning region which is based on the algorithms we present
in the previous sections and the
algorithm presented in
[
78
, Section 5]
.
The algorithm presented in
[
78
, Section 5]
draws ideas from a hierarchical clustering technique
[
217
,
158
]
.
Without loss of generality, we consider the parity objectives
\text{Parity}(p)
where

p:V\rightarrow\{0,1,\dots,2d\}
.
In the symbolic setting, instead of
p
, we get the sets
\mathcal{P}_{\geq i}=\{v\in V\mid p(v)\geq i\}
where
(1\leq i\leq 2d)
as part of the input.
We abbreviate the family
\{\mathcal{P}_{\geq i}\mid 1\leq i\leq 2d\}
as
(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{\leq m}=V\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\geq m+1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m}=\mathcal{P}_{\geq m}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\geq m+1}
.
Given an MDP
P
, let
P_{i}
denote the MDP obtained by removing
\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq i-1})
, the set
of vertices with priority less than
i
and its random attractor.
A MEC
M
is a
winning MEC
in
P_{i}
if there exists a vertex
u\in M
such that
p(u)=i
and
i
is even, i.e., the smallest priority in the MEC is even. Let
\mathsf{WE}_{i}
be the union of
vertices of winning maximal end-components in
P_{i}
, and let
\mathsf{WE}=\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq 2d}\mathsf{WE}_{i}
.
Lemma
37.5
says that computing
\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Parity}(p))
is equivalent to computing almost-sure
reachability of
\mathsf{WE}
. Intuitively, player 1 can infinitely often satisfy the parity condition after
reaching an end-component which satisfies the parity condition.

37.5
[
78
]
.

We have
\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Parity}(p))=\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Reach}(\mathsf{WE}))
.

Thus, we describe in Algorithm
8.7
how to compute
\mathsf{WE}
symbolically.

37.2.1
Algorithm Description.

The algorithm uses a key idea which we describe first.
Recall that
P_{i}
denotes the MDP obtained by removing
\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq i-1})
, i.e., the set
of vertices with priority less than
i
and its random attractor.

Key Idea.

If
u,v
are in a MEC in
P_{i}
, then they are in the same MEC in
P_{i-1}
.
The key idea implies that if a vertex is in a winning MEC of
P_{i}
, it is also in a winning MEC of

P_{i-1}
.
Intuitively, this holds due to the following two facts:
(1) Because
P_{i-1}
contains all edges and
vertices of
P_{i}
the MECs
P_{i}
are still strongly connected in
P_{i-1}
.
(2) Because
\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq i-1})
makes sure that no MEC
M
in
P_{i}

has a random vertex with an edge leaving
P_{i}
in
M
, the same is true for the set
M
in
P_{i-1}
.

We next present the recursive algorithm
WinParityEC(
P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j
)
which,
for a MDP
P
, computes the set
\bigcup_{i\leq\ell\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{i}
of winning MECs for priorities between
i
and
j
.

1.

Base Case:
If
j<i
, return
\emptyset
.

2.

Compute
m\leftarrow\lceil(i+j)/2\rceil
.

3.

Compute the MECs of
P_{m}=V\setminus\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq m-1})
and for each MEC
M\in P_{m}

compute the minimal priority
min
among all vertices in that MEC.

4.

For each MEC
M
:

•

If
min
is even then add
M
to the set
W
of vertices in winning MECs.

•

If
min
is odd we recursively call
WinParityEC(
P^{u},(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},min+1,j
)
where
P^{u}
is the sub-MDP containing only vertices and edges inside
M
.
This call applies the key idea and refines the MECs of
P_{m}

and computes the set
\bigcup_{min+1\leq\ell\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{\ell}
.

5.

Call
WinParityEC(
P^{\ell},(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,m-1
)
where
P^{\ell}
is the MDP where all MECs in
P_{m}
are
collapsed into a single vertex and thus only the edges outside the MECs of
P_{m}
are considered.
This call computes the set
\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq m-1}\mathsf{WE}_{k}
.

We initialize
WinParityEC(
P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j
)
with
WinParityEC(
P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},0,2d
)
.

Algorithm
8.7
is the formal version of the sketched algorithm.

Procedure

WinParityEC(
P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j
)

1

Input:

P=(V,E,\langle V_{1},V_{R}\rangle,\delta),(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j

W\leftarrow\emptyset

2

if

j<i

then

return

W

3

m\leftarrow\lceil(i+j)/2\rceil

4

X_{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq m-1})

5

Z_{m}\leftarrow V\setminus X_{m}
;
E_{m}\leftarrow E\cap(Z_{m}\times Z_{m})

6

P^{\prime}\leftarrow(Z_{m},E_{m},\langle V_{1}\cap Z_{m},V_{R}\cap Z_{m}\rangle,\delta)

7

for

M\leftarrow\mathsf{ComputeMECs}(P^{\prime})

do

8

9

min\leftarrow minPriority(M)

10

if

min
is even

then

11

W\leftarrow W\cup M

12

else

13

V^{u}\leftarrow M\setminus\mathsf{Attr}_{R}^{P}(\mathcal{P}_{min})

14

P^{u}\leftarrow(V^{u},(V^{u}\times V^{u})\cap E,\langle V_{1}\cap V^{u},V_{R}\cap V^{u}\rangle,\delta)

15

W\leftarrow W\cup\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P^{u},(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},min+1,j$}}\textnormal{{)}}

/*
MDP with MECs collapsed is
P^{\ell}
in the text */

for

M\leftarrow\mathsf{ComputeMECs}(P^{\prime})

do

CollapseEC(
M,P
)

16

W\leftarrow W\cup\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,m-1$}}\textnormal{{)}}

17

return

W

18

Algorithm 8.7

Compute WE of an MDP
P

37.2.2
Correctness and Number of Symbolic Steps.

In this section, we argue that Algorithm
8.7
is correct and bound the number of symbolic
steps and the symbolic space usage.
A key difference in the analysis of Algorithm
8.7
and
[
78
]

is that we aim for a symbolic step bound that is independent of the number of edges in
P

and, thus, we cannot use the argument from
[
78
]
which charges the cost of each recursive call
to the edges of
P
.
The key argument in
[
78
]
is that the sets of edges in the different branches of the recursions do not overlap.
For vertices, it is not that simple, as we do not entirely remove vertices that appear in a MEC but merge the MEC and represent it by a single vertex.
That is, a vertex can appear in both
P^{\ell}
and in
P^{u}
corresponding to the MEC.
To accomplish our symbolic step bound we adjusted the algorithm.
At Line
8.7
we
always
remove the minimum priority vertices
instead of removing the vertices with priority
m
to ensure that we remove at least one vertex.
Intuitively, by always removing at least one vertex from a MEC we ensure that the total number of vertices processed
at each recursion level does not grow.
Note that these changes of the algorithm do not affect the correctness argument of
[
78
]
as we always compute the same sets
\mathsf{WE}_{m}

but avoid calls to
WinParityEC(
\cdot
)
with no progress on some MECs.

37.6
Correctness.

Algorithm
8.7
returns the set of winning end-components
\mathsf{WE}
.

Proof.

The correctness of the algorithm is by induction on
j-i

for the induction hypothesis

\bigcup_{i\leq\ell\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{\ell}\subseteq\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j$}}\textnormal{{)}}\subseteq\bigcup_{1\leq\ell\leq 2d}\mathsf{WE}_{\ell}
.

First consider the induction base cases:
If
j>i
, the algorithm correctly returns the empty set.
Next, consider the induction step.
Assume that the results hold for
j-i\leq k
, and we consider
j-i=k+1
.
If
m
is even, then

\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{k}=\mathsf{WE}_{m}\cup\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq m-1}\mathsf{WE}_{k}\cup\bigcup_{m+1\leq k\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{k},

otherwise (
m
is odd), then

\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{k}=\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq m-1}\mathsf{WE}_{k}\cup\bigcup_{m+1\leq k\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{k},

Consider an arbitrary winning MEC
M_{k}
in
P_{k}
, i.e., the lowest even priority is
k
.
We consider the following cases.

1.

For all
k\geq m
we have that
M_{k}
is contained in a MEC
M_{m}
of
P_{m}
.
Additionally, no random vertex in
M_{m}
can have an edge leaving
M_{m}
and thus no
random vertex in
M_{k}
can have a random edge leaving
M_{m}
.
Moreover, for the minimum priority
min
of
M_{m}
we have
k\geq min\geq m
.
If
min
is even then
M_{m}
is itself winning and thus
M_{k}\subseteq\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j$}}\textnormal{{)}}
and
M_{m}\subseteq\bigcup_{1\leq\ell\leq 2d}\mathsf{WE}_{\ell}
(note that it might be that
min>j
).

If
min
is odd

M_{k}
is a winning MEC of
P
iff
it is a winning MEC of
P^{u}
and thus by the induction
hypothesis
M_{k}\subseteq\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P^{u},(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},min+1,j$}}\textnormal{{)}}
.
It follows that also
M_{k}\subseteq\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j$}}\textnormal{{)}}
.

2.

For
k<m
consider a MEC
M_{k}
in
P_{k}
. If
M_{k}
contains a vertex
v
that
belongs to a MEC
M_{m}
of
P_{m}
, then
M_{m}\subset M_{k}
(i.e., all vertices of the
MEC in
P_{m}
of
v
also belong to
M_{k}
and
M_{k}
has at least one additional vertex
with priority
<m
).
We thus have that for
k\leq m
the winning MECs
M_{k}
in
P_{k}
are in one-to-one correspondence
with the winning MECs
M^{\prime}_{k}
of the modified MDP where all MECs of
P_{m}
are collapsed.
From the induction hypothesis
it follows that
\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq m-1}\mathsf{WE}_{k}=\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,m-1$}}\textnormal{{)}}

Hence,
\bigcup_{i\leq k\leq j}\mathsf{WE}_{k}\subseteq\textnormal{{WinParityEC(}}\textnormal{\emph{$P,(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,j$}}\textnormal{{)}}\subseteq\bigcup_{1\leq\ell\leq 2d}\mathsf{WE}_{\ell}
. The statements follows from setting
i=0
and
j=2d
.
∎

37.7
Symbolic Steps.

The total number of symbolic operations for Algorithm
8.7
is
O(\mathbf{MEC}\cdot\log d)
for

0<\epsilon\leq 0.5
.

Proof.

Given an MDP
P
with
n
vertices and
d
priorities, let us denote by

T(n,x)
the number of symbolic steps of Algorithm
8.7
at recursion depth
x

and with
T_{M}(n)
the number of symbolic steps incurred by the symbolic MEC Algorithm.
As shown in
[
78
]
, note that the recursion depth of Algorithm
8.7

is in
O(\log d)
because we recursively consider either
(min+1,j)
or
(i,m-1)
where
min\geq m=\lceil(i+j/2)\rceil
until
j>i
, where
j=2d
initially.
First, we argue why there exists
c>0
such that

\displaystyle T(n,x)\leq c\cdot T_{M}(n)+\left(\sum_{i=1,\dots,t}T\left(n_{i},x-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle+T\left(n-\left(\sum_{i=1,\dots,t}n_{i}\right)+t,x-1\right)\text{ if }x>1

\displaystyle T(n,0)\leq c.

The attractors computed at Line
8.7
and Line
8.7

can be done in
O(n)
symbolic steps as the set of vertices in the attractors are all disjunct.
Clearly, this is cheaper than computing the MEC decomposition.
To extract the minimum priority of a set of nodes
X\subseteq V
we apply a binary search procedure which takes
O(\log d)
symbolic steps at Line
8.7
.
Note that when
\log d>n
we cannot charge the cost to computing the MEC decomposition.
Thus, we argue in Claim
37.8
that the total number of symbolic steps for Line
8.7
in Algorithm
8.7
is less than
O(n\log d)
.
The rest of the symbolic steps in Algorithm
8.7
, (except the recursive calls and
computing the MEC decomposition) in Algorithm
8.7
can be done in a constant amount of symbolic steps.

Note that when
x=0
, i.e., in the case
j<i
, we only need a constant amount of symbolic steps.

Let
t
be the number of MECS in
P^{\prime}
.
When
x>0
, consider the following argumentation for the
number of symbolic steps of the recursive calls:

•

WinParityEC(
P^{u},(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},min,j
)
:
We perform the recursive call for each MEC
M_{i}\in P^{\prime}

(1\leq i\leq t)

where the vertex with minimum priority is odd.
The total cost incurred by all such recursive calls is
\sum_{i=1,\dots,t}T(n_{i},x-1)
where
n_{i}\leq|M_{i}|-1
because we always remove the
vertices with minimum priority at Line
8.7
.

•

WinParityEC(
P^{\ell},(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d},i,m-1
)
:

P^{\ell}
consists of the vertices representing the collapsed MECs, the vertices not
in
P^{\prime}
and the vertices which are not in a MEC of
P^{\prime}
.
The number of vertices in
P^{\ell}
is thus
n_{\ell}=n-\sum_{i=1,\dots,t}|M_{i}|+t

and we obtain
T(n_{\ell},x-1)
.

Note that
\sum_{i=1,\dots,t}n_{i}+n_{\ell}\leq n
.
We choose
c
such that
cT_{M}(n)
is greater than the number of symbolic steps for computing
the MECs twice and the rest of the work in the current iteration of Algorithm
8.7
.
It is straightforward to show that

T(n,d)=O(T_{M}(n)\log d)
.

The following claim shows that the total number of symbolic steps incurred by
Line
8.7
for all calls to
WinParityEC(
\cdot
)
is only
O(n\log d)
.

37.8
.

The total amount of symbolic steps used by Line
8.7
is in

O(n\log d)
.

Proof.

To obtain the set of vertices with minimum priority from a set of vertices
X\subseteq V
the function

\mathsf{MinPriority}_{(}X)
performs a binary search using the sets
(\mathcal{P}_{\geq k})_{1\leq k\leq 2d}
.
This can be done in
O(\log d)
many symbolic steps.
To prove that the number of symbolic steps used by Line
8.7
in
total is in

O(n\log d)
note that each time the function is performed we either:
(i) Remove all
vertices in
M
, and we never perform the function on the vertices in
M
again. We charge the cost to an arbitrary vertex in
M
.
(ii) Remove at least one vertex at Line
8.7
and we never perform
the function on a MEC containing this vertex again. We charge the cost to this vertex.
As there are only
n
vertices we obtain that the total amount of symbolic steps used by
Line
8.7
is in
O(n\log d)
.
∎

Using Claim
37.8
we conclude the proof for the symbolic step bound of Algorithm
8.7
.
∎

37.9
.

Algorithm
8.7
uses
O(\mathit{space}(\mathbf{MEC})+\log n\log d)
space.

Proof.

Let
P
be an MDP
n
vertices and a parity objective with
d
priorities.
We denote with
\mathit{space}(\mathbf{MEC})
the symbolic space used by the algorithm that computes the MEC decomposition.
Observe that all computation steps in Algorithm
8.7
need constant space except
for the recursions and computing the MEC decomposition.
Both at Line
8.7
and Line
8.7

we first compute the MEC decomposition and then, to minimize extra space,
we output one MEC after the other by returning each SCC found given the set of vertices in nontrivial MECs.
Note that we only require logarithmic space to maintain the state of the SCC algorithm
[
74
]
.
As argued in
[
78
]
the recursion depth of Algorithm
8.7
is
O(\log d)
.
Thus, we need
O(\log n\log d)
space for maintaining the state of the SCC algorithm at Line
8.7
until we reach a leaf of the recursion tree.
At each
recursive call, we need additive
O(\mathit{space}(\mathbf{MEC}))
space to compute the MEC
decomposition of
P
. This yields the claimed space bound.
∎

Given an MDP, we first compute the set
\mathsf{WE}
with Algorithm
8.7
which is correct
due to Proposition
37.6
.
We instantiate
\mathbf{MEC}
and
\mathit{space}(\mathbf{MEC})
in Proposition
37.7
and
Proposition
37.9
respectively with Theorem
36.16
and thus need

O(n^{2-\epsilon}\log n\log d)
symbolic steps and
O(n^{\epsilon}\log n+\log n\log d)

(where
0<\epsilon\leq 0.5
) symbolic space for computing
\mathsf{WE}
.
Then, we compute almost-sure reachability of
\mathsf{WE}
with Theorem
37.4
.
Finally, using Lemma
37.5
we obtain the following theorem.

37.10
.

The set
\langle\!\langle\text{1}\rangle\!\rangle_{a.s.}(\text{Parity}(p))
of an MDP
P
can be computed with
\widetilde{O}(n^{2-\epsilon})

many symbolic operations and
\widetilde{O}(n^{\epsilon})
symbolic space for
0<\epsilon\leq 0.5
.

38
Conclusion

We present a faster symbolic algorithm for the MEC decomposition. Furthermore, we
improve the fastest symbolic algorithm for verifying MDPs with
\omega
-regular properties. There are
several interesting directions for future work. On the practical side, implementations and
experiments with case studies is an interesting direction.
On the theoretical side, improving upon the
\widetilde{O}(n^{1.5})
bound for MECs is an interesting open
question which would also, using our work, improve the presented algorithm for verifying

\omega
-regular properties of MDPs.

Chapter 9
Conclusion

In the thesis, we examine instances of central problems in model-checking and reactive synthesis.
Chapters
3
–
6
provide
explicit algorithms
for problems with
widely-considered objectives like mean-payoff parity objectives, Streett objectives,
bounded liveness objectives and variants of reachability objectives.
Chapters
7
–
8
provide
symbolic algorithms
for
problems with parity objectives in game graphs and MDPs.
The careful transfer of sophisticated modern graph algorithmic techniques to instances of these central problems
provides the new improved algorithms.

We conclude with concrete ideas for follow-up work.

Implementation and Experiments.

Even though the discovery of improved theoretical algorithms is an important problem-solving challenge we must also implement
them:
The implementation of a theoretic algorithm removes
semantic gaps between code and pseudocode and meaningful experiments offer valuable insights
into how an algorithm performs in the real world
[
206
]
.

•

For Streett objectives in graphs and MDPs and parity objectives in games implementations
of explicit algorithms
[
118
,
50
]
and symbolic
algorithms
[
205
,
176
,
93
]
exist. An interesting direction for
future work is to implement the algorithms for parity objectives in MDPs
introduced in Chapter
8
.

•

For problems with mean-payoff parity objectives in games our theoretical result in
Chapter
3
is improved to a
pseudo-quasi-polynomial running time
[
115
]

and it is important future work to determines which algorithms perform best in practice
in both the explicit model and the symbolic model of computation.

•

For bounded liveness objectives, implementations of the algorithms is future work.

•

For the MEC-decomposition in MDPs, implementations for explicit
algorithms exist
[
225
]
but for symbolic algorithms it is important future work.

Theoretical follow-up question.

Recently, a breakthrough for deterministic dynamic algorithms
[
207
,
102
,
32
]

yielded faster deterministic dynamic algorithms for many central graph-theoretic problems and it is
an important open question if the techniques are useful for improved
deterministic
algorithms
for MEC decomposition in MDPs or Streett objectives in graphs.
For symbolic algorithms improving upon the
\widetilde{O}(n^{1.5})
bound for MEC decomposition is an
interesting open question.
For explicit algorithms any improvement upon the
\widetilde{O}(n^{2.5})
time algorithm in graphs and the

O(n^{2}d)
time algorithms in games for bounded Büchi objectives is interesting.
Sub-cubic time
deterministic
algorithm for bounded Büchi
objectives in graphs discussed in Chapter
5
are interesting future work.
Additionally, any conditional lower bounds for bounded Büchi objectives in
games would separate
the objectives from Büchi objectives. Any improvement upon the long-standing

deterministic

O(mn^{2/3})
time algorithm for MEC decomposition
[
91
]
is interesting.
Finally, providing a
polynomial time
algorithm for computing the winning set of parity or
mean-payoff objectives in games is a major theoretical open problem and a formidable task for
future work.
